--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Let me analyze the mission carefully:

**Before switch time**: Score increases by 1 for every robot that is **stationary**. So robots should be **moving**.
**After switch time**: Score increases by 1 for every robot that is **moving**. So robots should be **stationary**.

The switch is signaled by a **GREEN wall lighting up**. The floor is uniformly GRAY throughout.

**Optimal strategy:**
1. Before switch: All robots move (exploration/random walk)
2. After switch: All robots stop (when they see green color)

The perception that signals the switch is: **seeing green color (color 2)** via the camera.

So the ideal FSM:
- **State 0**: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green is perceived
- **State 1**: Stop → stay stopped (or transition back if green disappears, but likely stays visible)

The current champion (score 11463.85) is just Exploration with no stopping - it's moving all the time. This is better than pure Stop (which would score ~24000 for all steps before switch). But we need to add the green-detection transition.

Let me estimate scores:
- Pure Exploration: ~20 robots × 1200 steps × 0.5 (half the time moving is penalized) = ~12000 (roughly)
- Actually: before switch (~500 steps) robots move = 0 penalty; after switch (~700 steps) robots move = 700×20 = 14000 penalty. So ~14000. Champion has 11463 which is better than I expected.

Wait - the champion is Exploration (moving all the time):
- Before switch (500 steps): 0 penalty (moving = good)
- After switch (700 steps): 20×700 = 14000 penalty
- Total ≈ 14000... but it's 11463, so maybe timing varies.

The ideal: move before switch, stop after seeing green.
- State 0: Exploration, transition on C=7 (perceive green, color=2) to State 1
- State 1: Stop, maybe transition back if needed (but keep stopped)

Let me design 14 machines:

1. **SURVIVAL**: Exact copy of champion
2-7. **EXPLOITATION**: 2-state FSMs with exploration → stop on green detection
8-14. **EXPLORATION**: Various topologies, different parameters

FSM designs:
- 2-state: Explore → (see green) → Stop
- 3-state: Explore → Stop → (optional recovery)
- Variations on probabilities and parameters

For the green color: clr=2 (green), C=7 condition with l=2.

Let me write 14 machines:

**FSM 1** (survival): Champion
**FSM 2-8** (exploitation): 2-state explore+stop, varying probability of green detection
**FSM 9-14** (exploration): 3-state variants, different approaches

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion is a 2-state FSM:
- **State 0**: Exploration (rwm=20, slow turning) → transition to State 1 when green perceived (p=1.0, always)
- **State 1**: Stop → transition back to State 0 when green NOT perceived (p=0.01, rarely)

This makes sense: robots explore until they see green, then stop. The rare return transition handles cases where green disappears from view.

Score 9871.70 is much better than the previous 11463.85. The key insight is confirmed: move before switch, stop after seeing green.

**Ways to improve:**
1. The return transition from Stop→Explore (p=0.01) might cause some robots to start moving again after stopping, hurting the score after switch. Maybe reduce this probability further or use a different condition.
2. The exploration turning rate (rwm=20) affects how well robots spread out and eventually see the green wall.
3. Maybe a 3-state FSM could help: Explore → Stop (on green) → with very rare escape back.

**Key considerations:**
- p=1.0 on green detection means robots stop immediately when they see green (good!)
- p=0.01 return probability from Stop → the robot might start moving again. This costs score post-switch. Lower probability would be better.
- rwm=20 is slow turning, which might help robots find the green wall systematically.

**Exploitation ideas:**
- Keep topology, vary rwm (10-40), vary return probability (0.001-0.02)
- Try p=0.0 return (no return from stop) - but then robots that stop before switch due to momentary green glimpse will be penalized. Actually since green only appears at switch, this should be fine!
- Wait - can robots see green before the switch? The problem says "one wall block lights up GREEN" at switch time. Before that, no green. So p=1.0 transition from explore to stop is safe (won't trigger before switch). And return probability from stop could be 0 to never leave stop.

Actually with p=0 return, robots that stop (because they saw green) never restart. This is optimal post-switch. But what if a robot sees green momentarily and then loses sight? With p=0 return it stays stopped. That's fine post-switch.

Let me try eliminating the return transition or making it conditional differently.

Note: `--cle` allowed values are {0, 4, 5, 6}. Fixed that error.

**My 14 machines:**

1. Survival (champion)
2-7. Exploitation: vary rwm, return probability
8-14. Exploration: different topologies, 3-state, etc.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion (score 7545.63) has improved significantly:
- **State 0**: Exploration (rwm=20) with TWO transitions:
  1. → Stop when green perceived (p=1.0) 
  2. → Stop with tiny probability (p=0.001) randomly
- **State 1**: Stop → back to Explore when green NOT perceived (p=0.005)

The second transition from State 0 (random stop with p=0.001) is interesting - it might help some robots stop "early" before the switch, but that would hurt pre-switch score. Actually p=0.001 is very small so it rarely fires. The key improvement over previous champion seems to be the combination of the random small stop probability AND the very low return probability from stop (0.005 vs 0.01).

Wait - the random stop from explore (p=0.001) could actually help: robots that accidentally stop before the switch are penalized, but after the switch, any robot that happens to have stopped due to this condition would already be stopped. However this is a minor effect.

**Key parameters to exploit:**
- rwm in State 0: currently 20, try 10-30
- p0x1 (random stop): 0.001, try 0.0005-0.002
- p1x0 (return from stop): 0.005, try 0.001-0.01
- Adding a second transition from Stop state
- 3-state variants

**Important insight**: The random stop transition from Explore (c=5, p=0.001) combined with the green perception makes the FSM more robust. Some robots might stop randomly and stay stopped if the green detection doesn't work for them.

**Improvements to try:**
1. Lower rwm (more straight movement = more likely to encounter green wall)
2. Even lower random stop probability
3. No return from stop at all (or near-zero)
4. 3-state FSMs with intermediate states
5. Different return conditions (neighbor count instead of color)

# ROUND 5/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion (score 6961.56) improved by reducing rwm from 20 to 10 (straighter movement helps robots find the green wall faster). The structure remains:
- **State 0**: Exploration (rwm=10, very straight) with two transitions:
  1. → Stop when green perceived (p=1.0)
  2. → Stop randomly (p=0.001)
- **State 1**: Stop → back to Explore when green not perceived (p=0.005)

**Key insight**: Lower rwm=10 (straighter movement) is better because robots cover more ground and reach the green wall sooner. Should I try rwm=1 (almost perfectly straight)?

**Exploitation ideas:**
- Try rwm=1, 5, 8 (even straighter)
- Vary p0x1 (random stop): 0.0005, 0.002
- Vary p1x0 (return from stop): 0.001, 0.003, 0.007
- Try removing the random stop transition entirely
- Try very low return probability

**Important consideration**: The random stop (p=0.001) helps robots stop before they see green IF they're already past switch time. But it also causes pre-switch penalty. At p=0.001 over ~500 steps, each robot has ~40% chance of randomly stopping at least once before switch. That's a significant cost. Maybe p=0.0001 would be better.

Actually wait - the random stop transition goes to State 1 (Stop), which then returns to Explore on p=0.005 when green NOT seen. So robots oscillate: explore → random stop → explore → ... But after the switch, once they see green they stop and only return at p=0.005. So the random stop is almost vestigial - let me try without it.

But the champion includes it, so it must help somehow. Perhaps it ensures robots eventually stop even if they never reach the green wall's camera range.

**Let me try:**
- Very low rwm (1-5) for even straighter movement
- Different combinations of random stop probability
- 3-state variants where robots that see green signal to neighbors

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Excellent progress! Score dropped from 6961 to 5451 with rwm=1 (almost perfectly straight movement). This makes sense: robots moving nearly straight will reach the green wall much faster, see it, and stop.

**Current champion analysis:**
- State 0: Exploration with rwm=1 (nearly straight) + two transitions: green→stop (p=1.0), random→stop (p=0.001)
- State 1: Stop → explore when green not seen (p=0.005)

**Why rwm=1 is so good**: Robots move nearly straight, quickly reach walls where the green block might be. Once they see green they stop immediately.

**What can still be improved?**

1. **The random stop (p=0.001)**: This helps robots stop even if they don't reach the green wall. But it also causes pre-switch stops. At p=0.001 per step, over 500 steps, probability of at least one random stop ≈ 40%. These robots stop, then return at p=0.005... complex dynamics.

2. **The return from stop (p=0.005)**: Very low already. Could try p=0.001 or p=0.003.

3. **What if stopped robots signal via LED?** Robots in Stop state could emit yellow LED, and exploring robots could transition to stop when they see yellow neighbors. This would create a cascade effect after the switch.

4. **3-state**: Explore → Stop (when green) → could have a state where robots stop AND emit a signal color, with neighbors detecting that signal.

Let me try:
- Exact champion copy
- Variants with even lower p0x1 (0.0005, 0.0001)
- Variants with different return probabilities
- LED-based signaling: stopped robots emit yellow, exploring robots stop when they see yellow
- Try rwm=1 with no random stop transition (only green detection)

**LED signaling idea**: 
- State 0: Explore, transition to State 1 on green (p=1.0)
- State 1: Stop + emit yellow LED, transition to State 0 on NOT green (p=0.005), also exploring robots see yellow and stop
- This requires: State 0 also has transition to State 1 on seeing yellow (C=7, l=4 for yellow... wait, yellow=4 but cle values are {0,4,5,6} for LED. Color 4=yellow for camera? Let me check: clr for GoToColor uses {1,2,3,4,5,6}, and C=7 condition uses l in {1,2,3,4,5,6}. So l=4 would be some color. But what LED color corresponds to what camera color? The LED colors are 0=off,4=yellow,5=magenta,6=cyan. But condition C=7 uses l in {1,2,3,4,5,6}. There might be a mapping issue - I'm not sure if l=4 detects yellow LEDs.

Actually I'll just try the signaling approach and also keep exploiting the champion topology.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The new champion (score 3647.07) is a 3-state FSM with LED signaling! Big improvement from 5451 to 3647. Let me analyze it:

- **State 0**: Exploration (rwm=1, nearly straight) + two transitions:
  1. → State 1 when green perceived (p=1.0)
  2. → State 1 randomly (p=0.001)
- **State 1**: Stop + **yellow LED (cle=4)** + two transitions:
  1. → State 0 when green NOT seen (p=0.005) — rarely returns to explore
  2. → State 1 itself... wait, n1x1 destination=1 means... with 3 states, leaving state 1, others are [0,2], so D=1 targets state 2!
  
Wait, let me re-read: "With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2."

So State 1 transitions:
- n1x0: D=0 → State 0 when green not seen (p=0.005)
- n1x1: D=1 → State 2 randomly (p=0.001)

- **State 2**: Exploration (rwm=1) + two transitions:
  1. → State 1 when green perceived (p=1.0)
  2. → State 1 when yellow perceived (p=0.8) — robots see stopped yellow-LED robots and stop!

This is brilliant! The cascade effect: robots that see green stop and emit yellow. Other robots see yellow and also stop. State 2 is a "secondary explorer" that stops on seeing either green OR yellow.

**Why State 2 exists**: Robots in State 1 that randomly escape via p=0.001 go to State 2 (not State 0). State 2 robots stop immediately when they see yellow LEDs (p=0.8), creating a cascade where stopped robots signal others to stop.

**Exploitation ideas:**
- Vary p2x1 (yellow detection probability): 0.8 → try 0.9, 1.0, 0.7
- Vary p1x1 (escape from stop to State 2): 0.001 → try 0.0005, 0.002
- Vary p1x0 (return to explore from stop): 0.005 → try 0.001, 0.003
- Vary p0x1 (random stop from State 0): 0.001 → try 0.0005, 0.002
- Try making State 0 also detect yellow (3 transitions)
- Try different LED colors for signaling

**Exploration ideas:**
- 4-state FSMs
- State 0 also detects yellow with high probability
- Different signaling colors (magenta=5, cyan=6)

Let me design 13 machines:

FSM: --nstates 3 --s0 0 --rwm0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 2 --n0x0 1 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 1.0 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 1 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.001 --s1 1 --cle1 4 --n1 2 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 7 --p1x0 0.005 --l1x0 2 --n1x1 1 --c1x1 5 --p1x1 0.001 --s2 0 --rwm2 1 --cle2 0 --n2 2 --n2x0 1 --c2x0 7 --p2x0 1.0 --l2x0 2 --n2x1 1 --c2x1 7 --p2x1 0.8 --l2x1 4

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Massive improvement! Score dropped from 3647 to 399.83! The champion now has State 0 with THREE transitions including yellow LED detection, creating a powerful cascade.

**Champion analysis:**
- **State 0**: Explore (rwm=1) + 3 transitions:
  1. → State 1 when green perceived (p=1.0)
  2. → State 1 randomly (p=0.001)
  3. → State 1 when yellow perceived (p=0.8) ← KEY ADDITION!
- **State 1**: Stop + yellow LED (cle=4) + 2 transitions:
  1. → State 0 when green not seen (p=0.005)
  2. → State 2 randomly (p=0.001)
- **State 2**: Explore (rwm=1) + 2 transitions:
  1. → State 1 when green perceived (p=1.0)
  2. → State 1 when yellow perceived (p=0.8)

**Why this works so well**: 
- State 0 robots stop when they see yellow LEDs from stopped robots
- This creates a cascade: a few robots stop (on green or random), emit yellow, neighbors see yellow and stop, cascade spreads
- Before switch: robots move, cascade doesn't start (no yellow emitters initially)
- At switch: green appears, first robots stop and emit yellow, cascade rapidly stops all robots

**Key difference from previous champion**: State 0 now also detects yellow (n0x2), so both State 0 and State 2 robots detect yellow. This means the cascade propagates faster.

**Ways to improve:**
1. Increase yellow detection probability in State 0 (0.8 → 0.9 or 1.0)
2. Increase yellow detection probability in State 2 (0.8 → 0.9 or 1.0)
3. Reduce return probability from stop (p1x0=0.005 → lower)
4. Reduce random stop from State 0 (p0x1=0.001 → lower to avoid pre-switch stops)
5. Make State 1 also detect yellow (to stay stopped when yellow is around)
6. Add 4th state for even more cascade control
7. Try making State 1 transition to State 2 more rarely (p1x1 lower)

**Critical insight**: The cascade must NOT trigger before the switch. Before switch, no yellow LEDs exist (assuming robots start in State 0 with no LED). The random stop (p=0.001) could cause early stops and yellow emission, which then cascades. This is the main source of pre-switch penalty. Reducing p0x1 to 0.0001 would help.

**Exploitation:**
- p0x1: 0.001 → try 0.0001, 0.0005
- p0x2 (yellow detect in S0): 0.8 → try 0.9, 1.0
- p2x1 (yellow detect in S2): 0.8 → try 0.9, 1.0
- p1x0 (return from stop): 0.005 → try 0.001, 0.002
- p1x1 (escape to S2): 0.001 → try 0.0005

- Make State 1 also detect yellow neighbors (stay stopped)
- 4-state FSM

## Reasoning

Score improved from 399.83 to 354.12. The champion now has:
- **State 0**: Explore (rwm=1) + 3 transitions: green→stop(p=1.0), random→stop(p=0.001), yellow→stop(p=0.8)
- **State 1**: Stop + yellow LED + 2 transitions: green-not-seen→explore(p=0.001), random→S2(p=0.0005)
- **State 2**: Explore (rwm=1) + 2 transitions: green→S1(p=1.0), yellow→S1(p=0.8)

Key change: p1x0 reduced from 0.005 to 0.001 and p1x1 from 0.001 to 0.0005. Lower return probabilities mean stopped robots stay stopped longer - this is critical!

**What can still improve?**
1. **p1x0** (return from stop when no green): 0.001 → try even lower: 0.0001, 0.0005. The lower this is, the less robots "accidentally" restart after stopping.
2. **p1x1** (escape to S2): 0.0005 → try 0.0001 or 0.0002
3. **p0x1** (random stop from S0): 0.001 → try 0.0001 to reduce pre-switch cascades
4. **p0x2/p2x1** (yellow detection): 0.8 → try 0.9, 1.0 for faster cascade
5. **Remove return from stop entirely**: p1x0=0 means robots never leave stop. But condition C=5 with p=0 would never fire... actually we need at least 1 transition from every state. Could use C=7 with l=2 (green) at very low p, meaning only restarts when green is gone AND probability fires. But actually if green light stays on, the condition "perceive green" would keep firing. Hmm.

Actually, wait - after the switch, the green wall stays lit. So if robots are in Stop (S1) with transition "when green not perceived → go to explore", and green IS visible, this transition fires rarely (only when robot turns away from green). If green is NOT visible (robot can't see the wall from its position), this fires at p=0.001 per step... that's still causing some restarts.

**Key insight**: Robots that stopped and can't see the green wall will occasionally restart. This is unavoidable with this structure. But very low p helps.

**What about making S1 transition only on C=5 (random) with very low p?** The champion already does this with p=0.0005. Going lower might help.

**Alternative**: Make S1 have NO random return, only return on seeing green (which would be rare if they stopped far from wall). But green perception means they DO see green, so if they see green they stay... wait, the condition is "perceive green → go to S0". That's wrong - we want them to STAY when green is visible. Actually the champion uses: S1 → S0 when green NOT perceived. That's condition C=7 with p=0.001 firing when green IS seen - no wait, C=7 fires when color IS perceived.

Let me re-read: C=7 fires when robot PERCEIVES the given colour. So `--c1x0 7 --l1x0 2` fires when robot sees green. But we want robots to return to exploring when they DON'T see green... 

Actually this means: S1 (stop) → S0 (explore) when green IS seen (p=0.001). That seems backwards. But maybe it works because: robots that see green confirm the switch happened and occasionally re-explore to spread the signal? Or maybe the simulator logic is different?

Wait - perhaps the intent is that after stopping (due to green), if the robot occasionally sees green again (still post-switch), it re-explores briefly. This doesn't make much sense for the mission. Maybe the p=0.001 combined with green perception means it almost never fires (since green might not be in view while stopped), effectively making stop permanent.

Regardless, the FSM works well. Let me focus on exploitation:

**Exploitation:**
- Reduce p1x0 further: 0.0001
- Reduce p1x1 further: 0.0001
- Increase yellow detection: 0.9, 1.0
- Reduce random stop p0x1: 0.0005

**Exploration:**
- 4-state FSM with dedicated "cascade" state
- Try making S1 use C=5 (random) instead of C=7 for return
- Try different return condition (neighbor count)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

The champion hasn't improved from round 8 to 9 (still 354.12). This is the final round, so I need to make the most of it.

**Champion structure recap:**
- S0: Explore(rwm=1) → S1 on green(p=1.0), → S1 random(p=0.001), → S1 on yellow(p=0.8)
- S1: Stop+yellow LED → S0 on green(p=0.001), → S2 random(p=0.0005)
- S2: Explore(rwm=1) → S1 on green(p=1.0), → S1 on yellow(p=0.8)

**Why is score still ~354?** With 20 robots and ~700 post-switch steps, if ALL robots stopped perfectly, score would be 0 post-switch, but pre-switch moving robots cost 0. The score of 354 means roughly 354/(20*700) ≈ 2.5% of post-switch robot-steps are still moving. That's very good but some robots still move after switch.

**What's causing remaining score?**
1. Pre-switch random stops cascading (p0x1=0.001 × 500 steps × 20 robots = ~10 cascade events)
2. Post-switch robots that don't see green or yellow quickly enough
3. Robots in S1 occasionally returning to explore (p1x0=0.001 on green perception)

**Key ideas for final round:**
1. **Eliminate pre-switch cascades**: p0x1 (random stop) → 0.0001 or even remove it (but need 1 transition minimum... we have 3 transitions so can remove random stop)
2. **Make S1 return even rarer**: p1x0 → 0.0001 and p1x1 → 0.0001
3. **Increase yellow cascade probability**: p0x2, p2x1 → 0.95 or 1.0
4. **Make S2 also have random stop**: adds another path to S1
5. **4-state with better cascade**: dedicated signaling state

**Critical insight for this final round**: The random stop from S0 (p=0.001) causes pre-switch cascades. Since green only appears at switch, before switch no yellow LEDs exist. But the RANDOM stop at p=0.001 creates yellow emitters pre-switch! These then cascade to stop other robots pre-switch. Removing this or making it tiny (0.00001) would help pre-switch score but might hurt post-switch coverage.

Actually: pre-switch, robots moving = score 0. Pre-switch, robots stopped = score penalty. So random stop pre-switch IS bad. But post-switch, robots that stopped randomly before the switch (and stayed stopped) are already scoring 0. The cascade from random stops pre-switch: robots randomly stop, emit yellow, neighbors see yellow and stop too - this is BAD pre-switch.

**Best fix**: Make p0x1 (random stop) = 0 effectively. But we need the transition... Actually we can just not include that transition and rely on green+yellow detection only. With 3 transitions, we could replace the random stop with another yellow detection or a neighbor-count condition.

Let me try removing random stop from S0 and replacing with a 4th transition or just 2 transitions.

Also: what if S2 has a random stop transition too? That creates more post-switch coverage.

**Most promising variants:**
1. Champion with p0x1=0.0001 (near-zero random stop)
2. Champion with p0x2=1.0, p2x1=1.0 (guaranteed cascade)
3. Champion with p1x0=0.0001, p1x1=0.0001 (almost never leave stop)
4. S0 with only 2 transitions (green + yellow), no random stop
5. Combining all improvements

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
